This gives that the integrand, and its first two derivatives, have locally uniform integrable bounds. this justifies differentiation under the integral and continuity of both derivatives. 
Integrating these bounds and adding the product-rule bounds for $Z^3/2$ yields
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d(Z_+^3)[H]} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}
    &\leqslant
    \left[
        \frac32+\frac1\pi\left(3+4+\frac12\right)
    \right]\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}
    \leqslant4\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}},\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d^2(Z_+^3)[H,K]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant
    \left[
        3+\frac1\pi\left(4+12+4+\frac23\right)
    \right]\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert K \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
    \leqslant10\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert K \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}.
\end{align*}
By positive homogeneity of degree three, rescaling extends this
twice continuous Fr\'echet differentiability to all of
$\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:block-regularization}
With $\phi$ and $\Phi_{a,W,T}$ as above, the following claims hold.
All constants below are independent of the matrix dimension.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*), ref=\theproposition(\alph*), leftmargin=*]

\item \label{prop:block-regularization-cutoff}
\textup{Cutoff bounds.}
The cutoff is well-defined and positive on $(-\gamma_Y,\infty)$.
For $y\geqslant0$, its denominator satisfies
$\phi(y)^{-1}\geqslant y$ and
$\phi(y)^{-1}\geqslant(\sqrt6-2)\gamma_Y\geqslant4\gamma_Y/9$.
Also $\phi(y)=y^{-1}$ for $y\geqslant\gamma_Y$.
For every symmetric $Z$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cutoff-square-weighted-bounds}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert \phi(Z^2) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\frac{9}{4\gamma_Y},
    \qquad
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert Z\phi(Z^2)^{1/2} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant1.
\end{equation}

\item \label{prop:block-regularization-square}
\textup{Regularity of the squared-argument cutoff.}
The following derivatives are of the whole map $Z\mapsto\phi(Z^2)$.
This map is twice continuously Fr\'echet differentiable
on $\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)$ and there is a universal constant $C>0$ such that, for all symmetric
$Z,\widetilde Z,H,K$ and $p\in\{2,\infty\}$,
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align}
\label{eq:cutoff-square-first-derivative}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d[\phi(Z^2)][H]} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}
    &\leqslant C\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}},\\
\label{eq:cutoff-square-second-derivative}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d^2[\phi(Z^2)][H,K]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant C\gamma_Y^{-2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert K \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},\\
\label{eq:cutoff-square-derivative-difference}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\bigl(d[\phi(Z^2)]-d[\phi(\widetilde Z^2)]\bigr)[H]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant C\gamma_Y^{-2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert Z-\widetilde Z \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{align}

\item \label{prop:block-regularization-representation}
\textup{Block representation and normalization.}
\prettyref{eq:regularized-scalar-functional} has the exact representation
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:block-trace-functional}
    \Phi_{a,W,T}(A)
    =\operatorname{tr}_{2n}\!\left[
      \begin{pmatrix}2T&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
      \phi\!\left(
        \begin{pmatrix}0&I_n+WM\\I_n+MW&0\end{pmatrix}^{\!2}
      \right)\right].
\end{equation}
For every symmetric direction $H$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:block-normalizations}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\begin{pmatrix}2T&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
       =\sqrt2\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},
    \qquad
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\begin{pmatrix}0&WH\\HW&0\end{pmatrix}} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
       =\ensuremath{\left\lVert {WH} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\leqslant\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{equation}

\item \label{prop:block-regularization-affine}
\textup{Affine dependence on $A$.}
For every symmetric direction $H$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:block-A-derivative}
    d_A\!\left[
      \begin{pmatrix}0&I_n+WM\\I_n+MW&0\end{pmatrix}
    \right][H]
    =-\beta\begin{pmatrix}0&WH\\HW&0\end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Its normalized Schatten $2$-norm is at most $\beta\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$.

\item \label{prop:block-regularization-safe}
\textup{Agreement on the safe set.}
For $W\succ0$, set $D=W^{-1}$. If
$(I_n+WM)(I_n+MW)\succeq\gamma_YI_n$, then
\[
    \phi\!\left((I_n+WM)(I_n+MW)\right)
    =\left((I_n+WM)(I_n+MW)\right)^{-1}
    =D(M+D)^{-2}D.
\]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
We prove each of the regularity bounds and the block identity and its affine dependence on $A$ in sequence.
 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part (a): cutoff bounds.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

For $y\geqslant0$, the inequality $x_+^3\geqslant3x/4-1/4$ gives
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    \phi(y)^{-1}
    &\geqslant y+\gamma_Y\left(
       \frac34\frac{\gamma_Y-y}{\gamma_Y+y}-\frac14\right)\\
    &=(\gamma_Y+y)+\frac{3\gamma_Y^2}{2(\gamma_Y+y)}-2\gamma_Y
    \geqslant(\sqrt6-2)\gamma_Y\geqslant\frac49\gamma_Y.
\end{align*}
Also $\phi(y)^{-1}\geqslant y$. For $-\gamma_Y<y<0$, its denominator is
greater than $y+\gamma_Y>0$.
The bounds in \eqref{eq:cutoff-square-weighted-bounds} follow by applying the same bound to each eigenvalue by using the commutativity of $Z$ and $\phi(Z^2)$.
For $y\geqslant\gamma_Y$, the correction vanishes and $\phi(y)=y^{-1}$.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part (b): regularity of the squared-argument cutoff.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

Write
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cutoff-square-denominator}
    \phi(Z^2)^{-1}
    =Z^2+\gamma_Y
       \left(2\gamma_Y(\gamma_YI_n+Z^2)^{-1}-I_n\right)_+^3.
\end{equation}
The argument of the positive part is a self-adjoint contraction.
Apply \eqref{eq:quadratic-resolvent-derivative} and \eqref{eq:quadratic-resolvent-derivative-2} with
$t=\sqrt{\gamma_Y}$ to obtain
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d[2\gamma_Y(\gamma_YI_n+Z^2)^{-1}-I_n][H]} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}
    &\leqslant2\gamma_Y^{-1/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}},\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d^2[2\gamma_Y(\gamma_YI_n+Z^2)^{-1}-I_n][H,K]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant4\gamma_Y^{-1}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert K \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}.
\end{align*}
Apply \prettyref{lem:positive-part-cubic} on the matrix
$2\gamma_Y(\gamma_YI_n+Z^2)^{-1}-I_n$, and then use the chain rule.
The correction term in \eqref{eq:cutoff-square-denominator} consequently
satisfies
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}-Z^2][H]} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}
    &\leqslant8\gamma_Y^{1/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}},\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d^2[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}-Z^2][H,K]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant56\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert K \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}.
\end{align*}
The second constant is $10\cdot2^2+4\cdot4=56$.
Since $d(Z^2)[H]=ZH+HZ$ and $d^2(Z^2)[H,K]=HK+KH$,
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-weighted-bounds} and H\"older's inequality give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cutoff-square-denominator-derivatives}
\begin{aligned}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\phi(Z^2)^{1/2}
        d[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][H]\phi(Z^2)^{1/2}} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}
    &\leqslant21\gamma_Y^{-1/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}},\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d^2[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][H,K]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant58\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert K \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In the first line, the two terms from $ZH+HZ$ contribute
$3\gamma_Y^{-1/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}$, and the correction contributes at most
$18\gamma_Y^{-1/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}$, using \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-cutoff} to bound the factors of $\phi(Z^2)^{1/2}$.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Inverting the denominator.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

\prettyref{lem:positive-part-cubic}, \eqref{eq:cutoff-square-denominator}, and positivity
of the denominator imply twice continuous differentiability on all of
$\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)$. Applying the inverse rule gives
\begin{align*}
    d[\phi(Z^2)][H]
    &=-\phi(Z^2)\cdot d[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][H]\cdot \phi(Z^2),\\
    d^2[\phi(Z^2)][H,K]
    &={}\phi(Z^2)\cdot d[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][K]\cdot \phi(Z^2)
           \cdot d[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][H]\cdot \phi(Z^2)\\
    &\quad+\;\phi(Z^2)\cdot d[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][H]\cdot \phi(Z^2)
           \cdot d[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][K]\cdot \phi(Z^2)\\
    &\quad-\;\phi(Z^2)\cdot d^2[\phi(Z^2)^{-1}][H,K]\cdot \phi(Z^2).
\end{align*}
In each product containing a first derivative of the denominator, insert
its two adjacent factors $\phi(Z^2)^{1/2}$ and apply
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-denominator-derivatives}.
The remaining outside factors have total operator norm at most
$9/(4\gamma_Y)$ by \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-cutoff}. In the last term, apply the second line of
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-denominator-derivatives} directly.
Thus
\begin{align*}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d[\phi(Z^2)][H]} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}}
    &\leqslant\frac94\,21\,\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{p}}},\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {d^2[\phi(Z^2)][H,K]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant\left(2\cdot\frac94\,21^2+\frac{81}{16}\,58\right)
        \gamma_Y^{-2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert K \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}.
\end{align*}
This proves \eqref{eq:cutoff-square-first-derivative} and
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-second-derivative}.
The square roots here are used only to bound products after differentiation;
no square root is differentiated.
Finally, integrate the second derivative along the segment from
$\widetilde Z$ to $Z$ to obtain
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-derivative-difference}.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part (c): block representation and normalization.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

The symmetric block matrix satisfies
\[
    \begin{pmatrix}0&I_n+WM\\I_n+MW&0\end{pmatrix}^{\!2}
    =\begin{pmatrix}
       (I_n+WM)(I_n+MW)&0\\
       0&(I_n+MW)(I_n+WM)
     \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Applying $\phi$ and taking the normalized
trace against $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2T&0\\0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$
proves \eqref{eq:block-trace-functional}.
The identities in \eqref{eq:block-normalizations} follow by taking the
normalized traces of the corresponding products with their transposes;
the last inequality uses $\ensuremath{\left\lVert W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant1$.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part (d): affine dependence on $A$.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

Since $M=aI_n-\beta A$, differentiation with $a$ and $W$ fixed gives
\eqref{eq:block-A-derivative}. Its norm bound follows from
\eqref{eq:block-normalizations}.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part (e): agreement on the safe set.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

The cutoff agrees with the reciprocal on the spectrum of
$(I_n+WM)(I_n+MW)$ under the stated assumption.
Since $I_n+WM=W(M+D)$, this product is $W(M+D)^2W$.
Its invertibility implies that $M+D$ is invertible, and taking the inverse
gives $D(M+D)^{-2}D$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:mixed-lipschitz-gradient}
Fix $\beta,\gamma_Y,C_{\mathrm{op}}>0$ and let
$C_1:=1+\beta C_{\mathrm{op}}$.
Let $A\in\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)$ satisfy
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert A \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{op}}$, let $a,\widetilde a\in[0,1]$, and let
$W,\widetilde W\in\mathcal D_n([0,1])$.
Let $T,\widetilde T\in\mathcal D_n(\mathbb R)$ satisfy
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\leqslant1$.
Set
\[
    M:=aI_n-\beta A,
    \qquad \widetilde M:=\widetilde a\,I_n-\beta A,
\]
and let $\Phi_{a,W,T}$ be the functional defined in
\eqref{eq:regularized-scalar-functional}.
Let $\nabla_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)$ be the gradient on
$\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)$ with respect to
$\langle H,K\rangle=\operatorname{tr}_n(HK)$.
Then there is a universal constant $C>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:mixed-lipschitz-bound}
\begin{aligned}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\nabla_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)
        -\nabla_A\Phi_{\widetilde a,\widetilde W,\widetilde T}(A)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} 
    \leqslant C\beta\Big[
       \gamma_Y^{-2}|a-\widetilde a|
       +\big(C_1\gamma_Y^{-2}+\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\big)
           \ensuremath{\left\lVert W-\widetilde W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       +\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T-\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\Big].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:cutoff-gradient-bound}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\nabla_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    \leqslant C\beta\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We will use the block representation of the test function. We use the fact that the block representation is an affine function of $A$, whose derivative is easy to compute. Extending this to the full test function is a matter of chain rule, followed by combining standard Fr\'echet derivative computations, Cauchy--Schwarz and the established regularity bounds. 
 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Differences of the block matrix.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

With $A$ fixed, let $\Delta$ denote the difference between
$(a,W,T)$ and $(\widetilde a,\widetilde W,\widetilde T)$, consistently across
all its uses, so that $\Delta M=(\Delta a)I_n$.
Note also that $\ensuremath{\left\lVert M \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},\ensuremath{\left\lVert \widetilde M \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C_1$.
For a symmetric direction $H$, \eqref{eq:block-A-derivative} and
\eqref{eq:block-normalizations} bound the normalized Schatten
$2$-norm of the $A$-derivative by $\beta\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$.
Then the discrete product rule gives
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align}
\label{eq:block-parameter-difference}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta\!\begin{pmatrix}
        0&I_n+WM\\I_n+MW&0\end{pmatrix} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
    &=\ensuremath{\left\lVert WM-\widetilde W\widetilde M \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
      \leqslant|\Delta a|+C_1\ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},\\
\label{eq:block-derivative-difference}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta\!\left(
       d_A\!\left[\begin{pmatrix}
          0&I_n+WM\\I_n+MW&0\end{pmatrix}\right][H]\right)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant\beta\ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{align}
Here the off-diagonal blocks are transposes of one another.
In particular, the $A$-derivative does not change when only $a$ changes.
 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{The scalar functional.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

Apply \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-square} in dimension $2n$, with
its matrix argument $Z$ replaced by
\[
    Z \;\;\gets\;\; \begin{pmatrix}0&I_n+WM\\I_n+MW&0\end{pmatrix}.
\]
By the chain rule,
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    d_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)[H]
    &=
    -\beta\,\operatorname{tr}_{2n}\!\left[
        \begin{pmatrix}2T&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
        \cdot
            d[\phi(Z^2)]
        \!\left[
            \begin{pmatrix}0&WH\\HW&0\end{pmatrix}
        \right]
    \right].
\end{align*}
By Cauchy--Schwarz for $\operatorname{tr}_{2n}$,
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-first-derivative} in dimension $2n$, \eqref{eq:block-normalizations},
\eqref{eq:block-A-derivative}, $\ensuremath{\left\lVert W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant 1$, and absorbing a factor of $\sqrt{2}$ into $C$,
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    |d_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)[H]|
    &\leqslant C\beta\gamma_Y^{-3/2}
       \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\begin{pmatrix}2T&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
       \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\begin{pmatrix}0&WH\\HW&0\end{pmatrix}} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\\
    &=\sqrt2\,C\beta\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {WH} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\\
    &\leqslant C\beta\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{align*}
Now keep $A$ and $H$ fixed.
In the chain-rule expression for $d_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)[H]$,
first change $T$ to $\widetilde T$, then change the direction
supplied to the derivative from the block matrix containing $W$
to that containing $\widetilde W$, and finally change the
evaluation point of $d[\phi(Z^2)]$ from the block matrix associated with $(a,W)$
to that associated with $(\widetilde a,\widetilde W)$.
The discrete product rule and Cauchy--Schwarz
for $\operatorname{tr}_{2n}$ then give
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    &|\Delta(d_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)[H])|\\
    &\quad\leqslant
    \sqrt2\,\beta\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {
        d[\phi(Z^2)]
        \!\left[
            \begin{pmatrix}0&WH\\HW&0\end{pmatrix}
        \right]
    } \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\\
    &\qquad+
    \sqrt2\,\beta\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {
        d[\phi(Z^2)]
        \!\left[
            \begin{pmatrix}
                0&(\Delta W)H\\
                H(\Delta W)&0
            \end{pmatrix}
        \right]
    } \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\\
    &\qquad+
    \sqrt2\,\beta\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {
        \left[\Delta\!\left(
            d[\phi(Z^2)]
        \right)\right]
        \!\left[
            \begin{pmatrix}0&\widetilde{W}H\\H\widetilde{W}&0\end{pmatrix}
        \right]
    } \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{align*}

The $\sqrt2$ factors come from
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2T&0\\0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
=\sqrt2\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$.

For the first and second terms, apply
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-first-derivative}.
For the third term, apply
\eqref{eq:cutoff-square-derivative-difference}.
The required bounds on the block matrices are
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\begin{pmatrix}0&WH\\HW&0\end{pmatrix}} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant\ensuremath{\left\lVert W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\begin{pmatrix}
        0&(\Delta W)H\\
        H(\Delta W)&0
    \end{pmatrix}} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    &\leqslant\ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta\!\begin{pmatrix}
        0&I_n+WM\\
        I_n+MW&0
    \end{pmatrix} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
    &\leqslant|\Delta a|+C_1\ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},
\end{align*}
by \eqref{eq:block-normalizations},
\eqref{eq:block-derivative-difference}, and
\eqref{eq:block-parameter-difference}.
Substituting these estimates into the three terms above and absorbing
$\sqrt2$ into the universal constant gives
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    |\Delta(d_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)[H])|
    \leqslant C\beta\Big[&
       \gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\\
       &+\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
          \ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\\
       &+\gamma_Y^{-2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert \widetilde{W} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
          \bigl(|\Delta a|+C_1\ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\bigr)
       \Big]\,\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{align*}
Finally, using $\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\leqslant1$ and $\ensuremath{\left\lVert W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant 1$ and $\ensuremath{\left\lVert \widetilde{W} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant 1$, we obtain
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    |\Delta(d_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)[H])|
    \leqslant C\beta\Big[&
       \gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
       +\gamma_Y^{-2}\bigl(|\Delta a|+C_1\ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\bigr)
       +\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert \Delta W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       \Big]\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{align*}
Taking the supremum over symmetric $H$ with $\ensuremath{\left\lVert {H} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}=1$ proves
\eqref{eq:mixed-lipschitz-bound} and then \eqref{eq:cutoff-gradient-bound} follows from the special case when $(\widetilde a,\widetilde W,\widetilde T) = (a, W, 0)$, so that $\nabla_A\Phi_{\widetilde a,\widetilde W,\widetilde T}(A) = 0$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:chaining-square-no-sqrtlog}
Fix $\beta,\gamma_Y,C_{\mathrm{op}}>0$, let
$C_1:=1+\beta C_{\mathrm{op}}$, and let $A\sim\mathsf{GOE}(n)$.
Let $\Pi_{C_{\mathrm{op}}}: \mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)\to\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)$ be the metric projection with respect to
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\cdot} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$, onto the closed convex set $\{M:\ensuremath{\left\lVert M \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{op}}\}$, and set
$\overline A:=\Pi_{C_{\mathrm{op}}}(A)$.
For the functional in \eqref{eq:regularized-scalar-functional}, define
\allowdisplaybreaks
\label{eq:chaining-centered-process}
\begin{align}
    \mathcal J
    &:= [0,1]\times\mathcal D_n([0,1])
       \times\{T\in\mathcal D_n(\mathbb R):\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}=1\},\\
    Z_{a,W,T}
    &:=\Phi_{a,W,T}(\overline A)
       -\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A[\Phi_{a,W,T}(\overline A)].
\end{align}
Then there are universal constants $C_{\mathrm{ch}},C_{\mathrm{tail}}>0$ such that
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:chaining-expectation}
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\left[\sup_{(a,W,T)\in\mathcal J}|Z_{a,W,T}|\right]
    \leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{ch}}\beta}{\sqrt n}
       \left((1+C_1)\gamma_Y^{-2}+\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\right),
\end{equation}
and, for every $p\in(0,1)$,
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:chaining-highprob}
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}_A\left\{\sup_{(a,W,T)\in\mathcal J}|Z_{a,W,T}|
    \leqslant\frac{C_{\mathrm{ch}}\beta}{\sqrt n}
       \left((1+C_1)\gamma_Y^{-2}+\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\right)
       +\frac{C_{\mathrm{tail}}\beta\gamma_Y^{-3/2}}{n}
          \sqrt{\log\frac2p}\right\} \geqslant 1 - p.
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
The proof will first utilize \prettyref{lem:mixed-lipschitz-gradient} on the test function induced by the regularized resolvent. After that, the proof is standard by invoking sub-Gaussian concentration via chaining and computing an upper bound on Talagrand's-$\gamma_2$ functional using the Dudley entropy integral.
 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Increment metric.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

Define the weights
\[
    \kappa_a:=C\gamma_Y^{-2},\qquad
    \kappa_W:=C\bigl(C_1\gamma_Y^{-2}+\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\bigr),\qquad
    \kappa_T:=C\gamma_Y^{-3/2},
\]
with a common universal prefactor large enough for
\eqref{eq:mixed-lipschitz-bound} and \eqref{eq:cutoff-gradient-bound}.
For $\theta=(a,W,T)$ and
$\widetilde\theta=(\widetilde a,\widetilde W,\widetilde T)$ in $\mathcal J$, set
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:rho-def}
    \rho(\theta,\widetilde\theta)
    :=\kappa_a|a-\widetilde a|
      +\kappa_W\ensuremath{\left\lVert W-\widetilde W \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
      +\kappa_T\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T-\widetilde T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{equation}
By \prettyref{lem:mixed-lipschitz-gradient},
\[
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\nabla_A\Phi_\theta(A)-\nabla_A\Phi_{\widetilde\theta}(A)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    \leqslant\beta\rho(\theta,\widetilde\theta)
    \qquad(\ensuremath{\left\lVert A \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C_{\mathrm{op}}).
\]
Integrating along line segments in this convex ball shows that
$\Phi_\theta-\Phi_{\widetilde\theta}$ is
$\beta\rho(\theta,\widetilde\theta)$-Lipschitz there.



 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Sub-Gaussian increments.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

The preceding Lipschitz bound, the 1-Lipschitzness of
$\Pi_{C_{\mathrm{op}}}$, and GOE Herbst concentration
\cite[Lemma B.7]{davies2026potential} give, exactly as in
\cite[proof of Theorem B.9]{davies2026potential},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:chaining-increment-tail}
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\{|Z_\theta-Z_{\widetilde\theta}|>u\}
    \leqslant2\exp\!\left(
      -\frac{n^2u^2}{4\beta^2\rho(\theta,\widetilde\theta)^2}\right)
    =2\exp\!\left(-\frac{u^2}{2d(\theta,\widetilde\theta)^2}\right),
    \qquad d:=\frac{\sqrt2\beta}{n}\rho,
\end{equation}
for $u>0$ and $\theta\ne\widetilde\theta$.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Chaining bound.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

Continuity of $\Phi_\theta(\overline A)$ in $\theta$ and its uniform
bound $|\Phi_\theta(\overline A)|\leqslant9/(4\gamma_Y)$ from
\prettyref{prop:block-regularization-cutoff} imply, by dominated convergence,
that the centered process has continuous sample paths on the compact
set $\mathcal J$ and is therefore separable.
Since $Z_{a,W,-T}=-Z_{a,W,T}$, Talagrand's chaining
\cite[Theorem B.6]{davies2026potential} gives
\[
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal J}|Z_\theta|
    =\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal J}Z_\theta
    \leqslant C\gamma_2(\mathcal J,d).
\]

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Entropy integral.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

By~\cite[Theorem B.5]{davies2026potential} and
$d=(\sqrt2\beta/n)\rho$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:chaining-entropy-integral}
    \gamma_2(\mathcal J,d)
    \leqslant C\frac{\sqrt2\beta}{n}
       \int_0^{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal J,\rho)}
          \sqrt{\log N(\mathcal J,\rho,u)}\,du,
\end{equation}
where $N$ denotes the covering number.

To bound $N(\mathcal J,\rho,u)$, note that $C_1\geqslant1$ implies
$\kappa_a+\kappa_T\leqslant\kappa_W$, and hence
\[
    \operatorname{diam}(\mathcal J,\rho)
    \leqslant\kappa_a+\kappa_W+2\kappa_T
    \leqslant3\kappa_W.
\]
For $0<u\leqslant\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal J,\rho)$, cover each parameter
factor at radius $u/(3\kappa_W)\leqslant1$ in its respective norm.
Since $\rho$ is a sum metric and each weight is at most $\kappa_W$,
the resulting product cover has $\rho$-radius at most $u$.
The same small-radius volumetric bounds as in
\cite[proof of Theorem B.9]{davies2026potential} imply
\[
    \log N(\mathcal J,\rho,u)
    \leqslant(2n+1)\log\!\left(\frac{9\kappa_W}{u}\right)\,,
\]
for $0<u\leqslant\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal J,\rho)$. Consequently, extending the integral of this nonnegative upper bound to $9\kappa_W$ and using the same substitution $u=9\kappa_We^{-t}$ as in \cite[proof of Theorem B.9]{davies2026potential}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
    \int_0^{\operatorname{diam}(\mathcal J,\rho)}
       \sqrt{\log N(\mathcal J,\rho,u)}\,du
    &\leqslant
    \sqrt{2n+1}\int_0^{9\kappa_W}
       \sqrt{\log(9\kappa_W/u)}\,du\\
    &=\frac{9\sqrt\pi}{2}\kappa_W\sqrt{2n+1}.
\end{align*}
Substituting this into \eqref{eq:chaining-entropy-integral} yields
\[
    \gamma_2(\mathcal J,d)
    \leqslant C\frac{\beta\kappa_W}{\sqrt n}
    \leqslant\frac{C\beta}{\sqrt n}
       \left(C_1\gamma_Y^{-2}+\gamma_Y^{-3/2}\right),
\]
where the last step uses the definition of $\kappa_W$.
Together with the preceding chaining bound, this proves
\eqref{eq:chaining-expectation}.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{High-probability bound.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

By \eqref{eq:cutoff-gradient-bound} and the same line-segment and
projection argument, each $A\mapsto\Phi_\theta(\overline A)$ is globally
$\beta\kappa_T$-Lipschitz. Centering preserves this constant, and so does
taking the supremum. Thus
\[
    \mathcal Z(A):=\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal J}Z_\theta
                 =\sup_{\theta\in\mathcal J}|Z_\theta|
\]
is $\beta\kappa_T$-Lipschitz.
As in \cite[proof of Theorem B.9]{davies2026potential}, applying GOE Herbst concentration~\cite[Lemma B.7]{davies2026potential} again to this supremum gives
\[
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}\left\{\mathcal Z(A)-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A[\mathcal Z(A)]>u\right\}
    \leqslant2\exp\!\left(-\frac{n^2u^2}{4\beta^2\kappa_T^2}\right).
\]
Taking $u=2\beta\kappa_T n^{-1}\sqrt{\log(2/p)}$ and using \eqref{eq:chaining-expectation} proves \eqref{eq:chaining-highprob}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:option-c-block-resolvents}
Let $A \sim \mathsf{GOE}(n)$ and $A_{\mathrm{GUE}} \sim \mathsf{GUE}(n)$ independently.
For $t\in[0,1]$ and $z\in\mathbb C\setminus\mathbb R$, set
\[
    A_t=\sqrt t\,A+\sqrt{1-t}\,A_{\mathrm{GUE}}\,,
\]
and
\[
    R_t(z)=\left[zI_{2n}-
      \begin{pmatrix}
      0&I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta A_t)\\
      I_n+(aI_n-\beta A_t)D^{-1}&0
      \end{pmatrix}\right]^{-1}\,.
\]
For $b=\Im z\ne0$, we have
\begin{align}
\label{eq:option-c-goe-gue-resolvents}
    &\ensuremath{\left\lVert \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}} R_1(z)-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}} R_0(z) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       \leqslant\frac{2\beta^2}{n|b|^3},\\
\label{eq:option-c-gue-free-resolvents}
    &\ensuremath{\left\lVert \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}} R_0(z)
       -(\mathrm{id}_2\otimes E_{M_n})
       \left[\left(zI_{2n}-
       \begin{pmatrix}
       0&I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta S)\\
       I_n+(aI_n-\beta S)D^{-1}&0
       \end{pmatrix}\right)^{-1}\right] \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       \leqslant\frac{2\beta^4}{n|b|^5}.
\end{align}
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
We use the characterization of the $M_n(\mathbb C)$-valued covariances $\eta_1$ and $\eta_2$ of the $\mathsf{GOE}(n)$ and $\mathsf{GUE}(n)$ ensembles \cite[Remark 1.6]{jekel2025strong} to identify the difference between them, and then invoke \cite[Proposition 1.8]{jekel2025strong} and \cite[Theorem 2.8]{bandeira2023matrix} to get quantitative strong convergence of $A_{\mathrm{GUE}}$ to its $(M_n(\mathbb C),\eta)$-semicircular limit. After that, one can use the characterization of $R_t(z)$ as an affine function of $A$ to compute its time derivative, and apply the standard operator-norm bound on the resolvent to compute the error between the $\mathsf{GOE}(n)$ and $\mathsf{GUE}(n)$ ensemble.
 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{The free $\mathsf{GUE}(n)$ model.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

For every $H\in M_n(\mathbb C)$, the normalized GUE covariance is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-gue-covariance}
    \eta_2 = \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[A_{\mathrm{GUE}}HA_{\mathrm{GUE}}]
       =\frac1n\sum_{i,j=1}^nE_{ij}HE_{ji}
       =\operatorname{tr}_n(H)I_n,
\end{equation}
where $E_{ij}$ are the standard basis elements for $M_n(\mathbb C)$. The Hermitian coefficient matrices multiplying independent standard real Gaussians are
\[
    \mathcal A := \left\{\frac{E_{ii}}{\sqrt n}\right\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n} \bigcup \left\{\frac{E_{ij}+E_{ji}}{\sqrt{2n}}\right\}_{1 \leqslant i < j \leqslant n} \bigcup \left\{\frac{i(E_{ij}-E_{ji})}{\sqrt{2n}}\right\}_{1\leqslant i < j \leqslant n}\,.
\]
Replacing the standard Gaussians in front of the coefficient matrices by copies of freely independent standard semicirculars gives an $M_n$-valued semicircular variable with covariance $\eta_2$ \eqref{eq:option-c-gue-covariance} that describes the free limit via \cite[Proposition 1.8]{jekel2025strong}. By the proof of \cite[Propositon 1.8]{jekel2025strong}, all cumulants of $M_n(\mathbb C)$ but the first two vanish under the non-commutative conditional expectation $\mathrm{id}\otimes\tau$. The
affine (in $A$) block expression of $R_t(z)$ preserves this agreement \cite[Proposition 9.3.13]{mingo2017free}. The block resolvents consequently agree for sufficiently large $|z|$ by their moment series, and then on both half-planes by analytic continuation. This identifies the coefficient-defined free model in \cite[Theorem 2.8]{bandeira2023matrix} exactly with the free resolvent in \eqref{eq:option-c-gue-free-resolvents}.

For the block affine representation at $t=0$, the relevant coefficients vis-a-vis the representation in \cite[(1.1)]{bandeira2023matrix} are $A_0 = \begin{pmatrix}
       0&I_n+aD^{-1}\\
       I_n+aD^{-1}&0
       \end{pmatrix}$ and $A_{ij} := -\beta 
       \begin{pmatrix}
       0&D^{-1}\mathcal A_{ij} \\
       \mathcal A_{ij}D^{-1}&0
       \end{pmatrix}$.
For these matrices, the parameters $\sigma(A_{\mathrm{GUE}})$ and $v(A_{\mathrm{GUE}})$ can be straightforwardly bounded using the fact that $\ensuremath{\left\lVert D^{-1} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant 1$ along with the operator covariance calculation for $\eta_2$ in \eqref{eq:option-c-gue-covariance} with $H = D^{-2}$ and $H=I_n$ to be 
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    \sigma^2(A_{\mathrm{GUE}}) &= \ensuremath{\left\lVert \sum_{\mathcal A_{ij}\in\mathcal A}A^2_{ij} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} = \beta^2\ensuremath{\left\lVert \sum_{ij}\begin{pmatrix}
       D^{-1}\mathcal A^2_{ij}D^{-1}&0 \\
       0&\mathcal A_{ij}D^{-2}\mathcal A_{ij}
       \end{pmatrix} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \\
       &= \beta^2\ensuremath{\left\lVert \begin{pmatrix}
       D^{-2}&0 \\
       0&\operatorname{tr}_n[D^{-2}]I_n
       \end{pmatrix} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant_{\ensuremath{\left\lVert D^{-1} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant 1, \operatorname{tr}_n[D^{-1}] \leqslant 1} \leqslant \beta^2\,,
\end{align*}
and
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
    v^2(A_{\mathrm{GUE}}) &= \ensuremath{\left\lVert \mathop{{}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Cov}}}(A_{\mathrm{GUE}}) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} = \sup_{M\in M_n(\mathbb C)\,,\left\lVert{M}\right\rVert^2_F=1}\sum_{ij}\left|\mathsf{Tr}\left[A_{ij}M\right]\right|^2 \\
    &= \beta^2\sup_{M\in M_n(\mathbb C)\,,\left\lVert{M}\right\rVert^2_F=1}\sum_{ij}\left|\mathsf{Tr}\left[\begin{pmatrix}
       0&D^{-1}\mathcal A_{ij} \\
       \mathcal A_{ij}D^{-1}&0
       \end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}
       M_{11}&M_{12} \\
       M_{21}&M_{22}
       \end{pmatrix}\right]\right|^2 \\
       &= \beta^2\sup_{M\in M_n(\mathbb C)\,,\left\lVert{M}\right\rVert^2_F=1}\sum_{ij}\left|\mathsf{Tr}\left[D^{-1}\mathcal A_{ij}M_{21}\right] + \mathsf{Tr}\left[A_{ij}D^{-1}M_{12}\right]\right|^2 \\
       &\leqslant_{\sum_{ij}|\mathsf{Tr}[\mathcal A_{ij}H]|^2 = 1/n\left\lVert{H}\right\rVert^2_F, \left\lVert{M_{12}}\right\rVert_F^2 \leqslant 1, \left\lVert{M_{21}}\right\rVert^2_F \leqslant 1} \frac{2\beta^2}{n}\left|\ensuremath{\left\lVert D^{-1} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\right|^2  \leqslant \frac{2\beta^2}{n}\,. 
\end{align*}
Noting that $\widetilde{v}^4(A_{\mathrm{GUE}}) = v^2(A_{\mathrm{GUE}})\sigma^2(A_\mathrm{GUE})\leqslant\frac{2\beta^4}{n}$ and invoking \cite[Theorem 2.8]{bandeira2023matrix} proves \eqref{eq:option-c-gue-free-resolvents}.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{The GOE--GUE correction.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 
 The underlying entry covariances, without complex conjugation, differ by
\[
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[A_{ij}A_{kl}]
       -\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}[(A_{\mathrm{GUE}})_{ij}(A_{\mathrm{GUE}})_{kl}]
       =\frac1n\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}.
\]
Apply the Gaussian covariance interpolation lemma \cite[Lemma 4.11]{bandeira2023matrix} entrywise to every two Gaussians in $R_t(z)$, in real Hermitian coordinates. The variance differences in the directions $E_{ii}$, $E_{ij}+E_{ji}$, and $i(E_{ij}-E_{ji})$ are $1/n$, $1/(2n)$, and $-1/(2n)$. Combining the latter two directions gives the matrix-unit contraction below. By the affine dependence on $A$ provided by \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-affine}, the two second-derivative terms from the inverse rule cancel the factor $1/2$ in covariance interpolation. Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-covariance-interpolation}
    \frac{d}{dt}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}} R_t(z)
    =\frac{\beta^2}{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\!\left[
      R_t(z)\left\{\sum_{i,j}
       \begin{pmatrix}0&D^{-1}E_{ij}\\E_{ij}D^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}
       R_t(z)
       \begin{pmatrix}0&D^{-1}E_{ij}\\E_{ij}D^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}
      \right\}R_t(z)\right].
\end{equation}
The integrand is well-defined with bounded derivatives for fixed $b\ne0$. Using $\sum_{i,j}E_{ij}HE_{ij}=H^{\mathsf T}$, the matrix in braces equals
\[
    \begin{pmatrix}
       D^{-1}(R_t)_{22}^{\mathsf T}D^{-1}&(D^{-1})^2(R_t)_{21}^{\mathsf T}\\
       (R_t)_{12}^{\mathsf T}(D^{-1})^2&D^{-1}(R_t)_{11}^{\mathsf T}D^{-1}
    \end{pmatrix}\,.
\]
Transposition preserves operator norm, and each block has norm at most $\ensuremath{\left\lVert R_t \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}$. A $2\times2$ block matrix has operator norm at most twice its largest block norm, and since $\ensuremath{\left\lVert R_t(z) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant|b|^{-1}$, we have
\[
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert \frac{d}{dt}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}} R_t(z) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       \leqslant\frac{2\beta^2}{n}\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}\ensuremath{\left\lVert R_t(z) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}^3
       \leqslant\frac{2\beta^2}{n|b|^3}\,.
\]
Integrating $R_z(t)$ over $t\in[0,1]$ gives \eqref{eq:option-c-goe-gue-resolvents}. The lower-half-plane version of the GUE--free estimate also follows from the upper-half-plane version by taking adjoints.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:simpler-free}
The application of \cite[Theorem 2.8]{bandeira2023matrix} and \cite[Lemma 5.11]{bandeira2023matrix} was obstructed in the previous argument of \cite[\S B.6]{davies2026potential} by the use of $A\otimesI_k$, which has $v(A\otimesI_k)=\sqrt{k}\,v(A)$ by
\cite[Lemma 8.1]{bandeira2023matrix}.
In the present approach, using the regularizer $\phi$ applied to the two-block resolvent, one only incurs a factor $2$ in $v^2$.
While the previous tensor interpolation argument could've been adapted to this new regularized version of the functional $\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)$, this simplification allows for a much shorter argument.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:option-c-smooth-transfer}
Under the hypotheses of \prettyref{lem:option-c-block-resolvents}, every real $f \in L^1(\mathbb R)$ that is six times weakly differentiable, with all derivatives $f^{(j)} \in L^1(\mathbb R)$, satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-smooth-transfer}
\begin{aligned}
 &\left\|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A f\!\left(\gamma_Y^{-1/2}
       \begin{pmatrix}0&I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta A)\\
       I_n+(aI_n-\beta A)D^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\right.\\[-2pt]
 &\qquad\left.-(\mathrm{id}_2\otimes E_{M_n})
       \left[f\!\left(\gamma_Y^{-1/2}
       \begin{pmatrix}0&I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta S)\\
       I_n+(aI_n-\beta S)D^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\right)\right]
       \right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\\
 &\quad\leqslant\frac{C}{n}\left[
      \beta^2\gamma_Y^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^4\|f^{(j)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}
     +\beta^4\gamma_Y^{-2}\sum_{j=0}^6\|f^{(j)}\|_{L^1(\mathbb R)}\right]\, ,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where $C>0$ is a universal constant.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
For a self-adjoint operator $K$, scaling its resolvent gives
\[
    (zI-\gamma_Y^{-1/2}K)^{-1}
       =\sqrt{\gamma_Y}\,(\sqrt{\gamma_Y}zI-K)^{-1}.
\]
Therefore, the two resolvent bounds from \prettyref{lem:option-c-block-resolvents} become $2\beta^2/(n\gamma_Yb^3)$ and $2\beta^4/(n\gamma_Y^2b^5)$ for the block matrices rescaled by the spectral cutoff $\gamma_Y$. Now, choose any $u \in \mathcal S_\mathbb C^{2n-1}(1)$ and consider the (average) $u$-weighted empirical eigendensities with $A$ and $A_{\mathrm{GUE}}$
\[
    u^\mathsf{T}\left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A[R_{1}(z)]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mathrm{GUE}}[R_{0}(z)]\right)u = g_{\mu_A,u} - g_{\mu_{A_{\mathrm{GUE},u}}} \leqslant_{u^\mathsf{T} C u \leqslant \ensuremath{\left\lVert C \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}} \ensuremath{\left\lVert \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A[R_{1}(z)]-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mathrm{GUE}}[R_{0}(z)] \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant_{\text{\prettyref{eq:option-c-goe-gue-resolvents}}} \frac{2\beta^2}{nb^3}\,,
\]
and the $u$-weighted spectral measure with $S$ and $A_\mathrm{GUE}$ 
\[
    u^\mathsf{T} \left(\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mathrm{GUE}}[R_{0}(z) ]- (\operatorname{id}_2\otimes E_{M_n})R_S(z)\right) u = g_{\mu_{A_{\mathrm{GUE},u}}} - g_{\mu_S,u} \leqslant \ensuremath{\left\lVert \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_{\mathrm{GUE}}[R_{0}(z) ]- (\operatorname{id}_2\otimes E_{M_n})R_S(z) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant_{\text{\prettyref{eq:option-c-gue-free-resolvents}}} \frac{2\beta^4}{nb^5}\,,
\]
where $g_{\mu_{A_{\mathrm{GUE},u}}}, g_{\mu_{A_{u}}}, g_{\mu_S,u}$ denote the Cauchy--Stieltjes transforms for the subscripted operators with measure given by the empirical eigendensity reweighed by $u$\footnote{\,To see this, apply the spectral theorem to the respective operators, and take the quadratic form. Then, use the fact that $u$ is unit-norm to construct the $u$-reweighed empirical eigendensity.}. By \cite[Lemma 5.11]{bandeira2023matrix}, a Stieltjes-transform difference bounded by $Kb^{-m}$ gives a smooth-test difference at most $C_mK\sum_{j=0}^{m+1}\|f^{(j)}\|_1$. We apply this twice---once to the GOE--GUE pair with $m=3$, and the second time to the GUE--free pair with $m=5$. We conclude with a triangle inequality, followed by taking the supremum over unit-vectors $u$, which proves \eqref{eq:option-c-smooth-transfer}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:option-c-expectation}
Fix $D\in\mathcal D_n(\mathbb R)$ with $D\succeqI_n$ and let $a\in[0,1]$, $\beta>0$, $\gamma_Y>0$. Suppose that the spectral gap condition in the free limit
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-free-buffer}
    (I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta S))(I_n+(aI_n-\beta S)D^{-1})
       \succeq4\gamma_YI_n
\end{equation}
holds in $\mathcal M_n$. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-expectation-bound}
\begin{aligned}
 &\Big\|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\phi\!\left(
       (I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta A))(I_n+(aI_n-\beta A)D^{-1})\right)\\
 &\qquad- E_{M_n}\!\left[D(aI_n-\beta S+D)^{-2}D\right]
       \Big\|_{\mathrm{op}}
       \leqslant\frac{C}{n}\left(\beta^2\gamma_Y^{-2}
                             +\beta^4\gamma_Y^{-3}\right).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
In particular, for every diagonal $T$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-scalar-expectation}
    \left|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)
       -\tau_n\!\left[TD(aI_n-\beta S+D)^{-2}D\right]\right|
       \leqslant\frac{C}{n}\left(\beta^2\gamma_Y^{-2}
                             +\beta^4\gamma_Y^{-3}\right)\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},
\end{equation}
where $W:=D^{-1}$.
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
The main goal of the proof is to ``envelope'' (or sandwich) the function $\phi$ inside its range by two functions that satisfy the differentiability requirements of \prettyref{lem:option-c-smooth-transfer}. We then show that both the enveloping functions will lead to the the same free limit using the spectral-gap condition in \eqref{eq:option-c-free-buffer}. After that, it is a simple matter of invoking \prettyref{lem:option-c-smooth-transfer} on the sandwiching functions to ``lift'' them to sandwich the operator-valued function.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Smooth envelopes.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

To apply \prettyref{lem:option-c-smooth-transfer}, which requires derivatives through order six, we sandwich the reciprocal extension $\sqrt{\gamma_Y}x$ by smooth ``envelopes''. Basic algebra shows that the rescaled function
\[
    x\longmapsto\gamma_Y\phi\left((\sqrt{\gamma_Y}x)^2\right)
      =\left(x^2+
        \left(\frac{(1-x^2)_+}{1+x^2}\right)^3\right)^{-1}
\]
is even and independent of $\gamma_Y$. By \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-cutoff}, the image of $\gamma_T\phi(\cdot)$ lies in $(0,9/4]$ and equals $x^{-2}$ for $|x|\geqslant1$.
We set $f_{-}(x)$ to be a smooth function that is equal to $0$ on $[-1,1]$ and to $x^{-2}$ for $|x|\geqslant2$, and is the lower sandwich for $\gamma_Y\phi(\cdot)$. Similarly, $f_{+}(x)$ is a smooth function that is equal to $9/4$ on $[-1,1]$ and to $x^{-2}$ for $|x|\geqslant2$, and is the upper sandwich for $\gamma_Y\phi(\cdot)$. On $1<|x|<2$, interpolate smoothly between the respective inner formula and $x^{-2}$ using a weight in $[0,1]$ that is constant near the endpoints\footnote{\,An explicit such choice comes from taking any fixed small $0<\delta<1/2$, and a smooth and even function $g: \mathbb R \to [0,1]$ that is $0$ whenever $|x| \leqslant 1+\delta$ and $1$ whenever $|x| \geqslant 1+2\delta$. Then, set $f_{-}(x) = g(x)x^{-2}$ for $x\neq 0$ (and $0$ otherwise) and $f_{+}(x) = f_{-}(x) + (1-g(x))9/4$.}. The function $f_{-}(x)$ is at most $x^{-2}$ there, and the function $f_{+}(x)$ is at least $x^{-2}$. These two functions, therefore, sandwich $\gamma_Y\phi(\gamma_Yx^2)$ on $\mathbb R$ while being even and smooth.
Since $(-2,2)$ is a closed set on which both functions are bounded, and outside that they are equal to $x^{-2}$, both these functions are integrable. Furthermore, all their derivatives derivatives exist, are $0$ inside $[-1,1]$, are some bounded smooth function in $(-2,-1] \cup [1,2)$, and are equal to $ f^{(k)} = d/dx^{k} x^{-2} = \prod_{i=1}^k (-i+1) x^{-2-k}$ for $\mathbb R \setminus (-2,2)$, and so they are integrable. Consequently, $\sum_{j=0}^6 \left\lVert{f^{(j)}}\right\rVert_1 \leqslant C$ for some $C>0$. Lastly, note that the rescalings $\gamma_Y^{-1}f_{\pm}(x/\sqrt{\gamma_Y})$ sandwich $\phi(x^2)$ and equal $x^{-2}$ for $|x| \geqslant 2 \sqrt{\gamma_Y}$.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{The common free limit.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 
 \eqref{eq:option-c-free-buffer} implies that $aI_n-\beta S+D$ is invertible, since, conjugating by $D$ gives
\[
    (aI_n-\beta S+D)^2\succeq4\gamma_YD^2\succeq_{D \succ I_n}4\gamma_YI_n\,.
\]
Note that \eqref{eq:option-c-free-buffer} also implies that $I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta S)$ is invertible since $D\succeq I_n$, and its two squared singular-value operators  are unitarily conjugate\footnote{\,This simply means that there exists a unitary $U$, such that, for any invertible operator $Y$ in the algebra $YY^* = U(Y^*Y)U^*$.}.
This immediately implies that both diagonal blocks in the square of the free block matrix consequently
have spectrum in $[4\gamma_Y,\infty)$, since
\[
    (aI_n-\beta S +D)D^{-2}(aI_n-\beta S +D) = U(D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta S + D)^{2}D^{-1})U^* \succeq U(4\gamma_Y I_n)U^* \geqslant 4\gamma_YI_n\,.
\]
This means that $\mathsf{Spec}\left(\text{free block}\right) \notin (-2\sqrt{\gamma_Y},2\sqrt{\gamma_Y})$.
Both $\gamma^{-1}_Yf_{\pm}(x/\sqrt{\gamma_Y})$ agree outside the spectrum with $x^{-2}$ (which is $\phi(x^2)$ in this range) -
this means their (non-commutative) free expectations are identical.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Operator sandwich.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

We now invoke \prettyref{lem:option-c-smooth-transfer} on $f_{\pm}(x/\sqrt{\gamma_Y})$ and
multiply by $\gamma_Y^{-1}$, which gives
\[
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert \mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A[f_{\pm}(\gamma_Y^{-1/2}\mathcal B(A))]-\operatorname{id}_2\otimes E_{M_n}[f_{\pm}(\gamma^{-1/2}\mathcal B(S))] \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant \frac{C}{n}\left(\beta^2\gamma^{-1}_Y + \beta^4\gamma^{-2}_Y\right)\,,
\]
where $\mathcal B(\cdot)$ is the block matrix taken as input to $f$ in \prettyref{lem:option-c-smooth-transfer}. Multiplying by $\gamma_Y^{-1}$, the finite expected value of each rescaled envelope $\gamma^{-1}_Yf(\gamma^{-1/2}_Yx)$ is within $Cn^{-1}(\beta^2\gamma_Y^{-2}+\beta^4\gamma_Y^{-3})$ in operator norm of that common free conditional expectation.
Spectral functional calculus at each fixed block matrix, followed by
positivity of expectation, places the expected reciprocal extension
between these two matrix bounds. It therefore satisfies the same
operator-norm estimate. This uses pointwise ordering of scalar functions,
not operator monotonicity with respect to different matrix arguments.
Taking the upper-left block and applying the block identity and reciprocal
agreement from \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-representation} and
\prettyref{prop:block-regularization-safe} at the free endpoint proves
\eqref{eq:option-c-expectation-bound}.
The definition of $\Phi_{a,W,T}$, trace duality, and
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{1}}}\leqslant\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$ then give
\eqref{eq:option-c-scalar-expectation}, using that $E_{M_n}$ is
trace-preserving. We defer diagonal norm duality and the restriction of
the realized parameters to the section-final corollary.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:diagonal-Q2-controlled-in-l2}
Let $A \sim \mathsf{GOE}(n)$ and fix $0<\beta<1$, $0<\gamma_Y\leqslant\frac{(1-\beta)^4}{4(1+2\beta^2)^2}$. For all $n \geqslant n_0(\beta,\gamma_Y)$, uniformly over all
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diagonal-safe-set-conditions}
    \left\{D\in\mathcal D_n([1,\infty)) \mid D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta A+D)^2D^{-1}\succeq\gamma_YI_n\right\}\, ,
\end{equation}
the following holds
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:diagonal-DQ2D-controlled-in-l2}
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}_A\left\{\ensuremath{\left\lVert {
      E_{\mathcal D_n}\!\left[
        D(\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(D^{-1})I_n-\beta A+D)^{-2}D\right]
      -\frac{I_n}{1-\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(D^{-2})}
    } \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    \leqslant\frac{C\beta}{\gamma_Y^2\sqrt n}\right\} \geqslant 1-4e^{-n/4},
\end{equation}
for a universal constant $C>0$. In particular, on the same event, \eqref{eq:diagonal-DQ2D-controlled-in-l2} holds uniformly for $D=D(m)$ with $m\in\mathcal S_A(c)$ from \prettyref{def:good-points}, for any fixed $c>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-finite-calibration}
    \gamma_Y\leqslant\left(1+\frac{1+3\beta}{c}\right)^{-2}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
We first control the deviation of the regularized resolvent. Set $C_{\mathrm{op}}=3$ and $\overline A=\Pi_3(A)$ in \prettyref{thm:chaining-square-no-sqrtlog}, so that $C_1=1+3\beta\leqslant4$ and invoke \prettyref{prop:goe-norm-tail}.  Then, invoke \eqref{eq:chaining-highprob} with $p=2e^{-n/4}$. Since $\gamma_Y\leqslant1$ and $\sqrt{\log(2/p)}=\sqrt n/2$, 
\[
    \mathop{{}\mathbb{P}}_{A}\left\{\sup_{(a,W,T)\in\mathcal J}|Z_{a,W,T}| \leqslant\frac{C\beta}{\gamma_Y^2\sqrt n}\right\} \geqslant 1-2e^{-n/4}\,.
\]

Set a deterministic $W=D\succeqI_n$ and $a=\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(D^{-1})\in[0,1]$. \prettyref{lem:option-c-tap-free-endpoint} provides a spectral cut-off via the TAP subordination so that \prettyref{eq:option-c-free-buffer} holds, yielding \eqref{eq:option-c-scalar-expectation} as the free target. Consequently, for every diagonal $T$,
\[
    \left|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)
      -\frac{\operatorname{tr}_n(T)}{1-\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(D^{-2})}\right|
    \leqslant\frac{C}{n}
       \left(\beta^2\gamma_Y^{-2}+\beta^4\gamma_Y^{-3}\right)\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\]
Also, \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-cutoff} and \prettyref{prop:goe-norm-tail} give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:option-c-projection-mean}
    \left|\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(\overline A)-\mathop{{}\mathbb{E}}_A\Phi_{a,W,T}(A)\right|
    \leqslant\frac{9}{\gamma_Y}e^{-n/4}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},
\end{equation}
since the two observables agree when $\ensuremath{\left\lVert A \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant3$ and each has absolute value at most $9\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}/(4\gamma_Y)$.  
For fixed $\beta,\gamma_Y$, these two expectation errors are $o(\beta\gamma_Y^{-2}n^{-1/2})$ and so, on the chaining event, the triangle inequality yields
\[
    \left|\Phi_{a,W,T}(\overline A)
       -\frac{\operatorname{tr}_n(T)}{1-\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(D^{-2})}\right|
    \leqslant\frac{C\beta}{\gamma_Y^2\sqrt n}\,,
\]
uniformly over valid $D$ and all diagonal $T$ with $\ensuremath{\left\lVert {T} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}=1$, for all $n \geqslant n_0(\beta,\gamma_Y)$. Intersecting the chaining event with $\{\ensuremath{\left\lVert A \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant3\}$ gives probability at least $1-4e^{-n/4}$. On this intersection $\overline A=A$, and \prettyref{prop:block-regularization-safe} makes the reciprocal extension equal to $D(aI_n-\beta A+D)^{-2}D$ for every realized $D$ satisfying \eqref{eq:diagonal-safe-set-conditions}. Taking the supremum over $T$ and applying diagonal norm duality (see \cite[Proof of Lemma 4.25]{jekel2024pha}) proves
\eqref{eq:diagonal-DQ2D-controlled-in-l2}.

Finally, to restrict to the good set, choose $m\in\mathcal S_A(c)$ and set $D=D(m)$. Then, $a=\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(D^{-1})=\beta^2(1-\left\lVert{m}\right\rVert_2^2/n)$. Since $\ensuremath{\left\lVert aI_n-\beta A \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant1+3\beta$ on the same event, \eqref{e:FTAP} and \prettyref{def:good-points} give
\begin{align*}
    aI_n-\beta A+D
    &=\nabla_m^2\mathcal F_{{\mathrm{TAP}}}(m,\cdot)+\frac{2\beta^2}{n}mm^{\mathsf T}
      \succ cD\succeq cI_n,\\
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert (I_n+D^{-1}(aI_n-\beta A))^{-1} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
    &=\ensuremath{\left\lVert I_n-(aI_n-\beta A+D)^{-1}(aI_n-\beta A) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
      \leqslant1+\frac{1+3\beta}{c}.
\end{align*}
Therefore, \eqref{eq:option-c-finite-calibration} implies the product condition \eqref{eq:diagonal-safe-set-conditions} uniformly over $m\in\mathcal S_A(c)$.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds}
Let $D,a,\overline Q,\widehat Q$ be as in \eqref{eq:DahQ}.
Assume $\ensuremath{\left\lVert A \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant3$.
Then for every $m\in\mathcal S_A(c)$, both $\overline{Q}$ and $\widehat{Q}$ are positive definite and
satisfy
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),ref=\thetheorem(\alph*),itemsep=0.1em]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-Q}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert \overline Q(m) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},\ \ensuremath{\left\lVert \widehat Q(m) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant c^{-1}. \]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-DQ}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-weighted-Q-bounds}
 \ensuremath{\left\lVert D(m)\overline Q(m) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},\ \ensuremath{\left\lVert D(m)\widehat Q(m) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       \leqslant1+\frac{3\beta+3\beta^2}{c}.
\end{equation}
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-DQ2}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert D(m)\overline Q(m)^2 \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},\ \ensuremath{\left\lVert D(m)\widehat Q(m)^2 \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       \leqslant c^{-1}\bigl(1+(3\beta+3\beta^2)/c\bigr). \]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-DQ2D}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert D(m)\overline Q(m)^2D(m) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}},\ \ensuremath{\left\lVert D(m)\widehat Q(m)^2D(m) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}
       \leqslant\bigl(1+(3\beta+3\beta^2)/c\bigr)^2. \]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The proof is the same as \cite[Lemma 6.1]{davies2026potential}, except
that the gap now follows from
\[
 \widehat Q(m)^{-1}\succ cD(m)\succeq cI_n,\qquad
 \overline Q(m)^{-1}=\widehat Q(m)^{-1}+\frac{2\beta^2}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}}
                       \succ cD(m).
\]
In \cite[Lemma B.1]{davies2026potential}, use $W=D(m)^{-1}$ and,
respectively, $M=a(m)I_n-\beta A$ or
$M=a(m)I_n-\beta A-2\beta^2mm^{\mathsf{T}}/n$.
Both have $\ensuremath{\left\lVert M \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant3\beta+3\beta^2$.
The identities $DR=I_n-MR$ and $RD=I_n-RM$ give the weighted bounds.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz}
In the setting of \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds}, the following
Lipschitz bounds hold for the following functions of $m$ on $\mathcal S_A(c)$, with Euclidean distance on the
input $m \in \mathcal S_A(c)$ and normalized Schatten $2$-norm $\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\cdot} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$ on the output:
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),ref=\thetheorem(\alph*),itemsep=0.1em]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-Q}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-resolvent-endpoint-Lipschitz}
 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widehat Q} \right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}},\ \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\overline Q} \right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}}
                         \leqslant\frac{C_{\beta,c}}{\sqrt n}.
\end{equation}
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-Q2}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widehat Q^2} \right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\beta,c}}{\sqrt n}. \]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-DQ}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert {D\widehat Q} \right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\beta,c}}{\sqrt n}. \]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-DQQD}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert {D\widehat Q^2D} \right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\beta,c}}{\sqrt n}. \]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Follow \cite[Lemma 6.2]{davies2026potential}, replacing its derivative
bounds by parts (a)--(e) of \prettyref{lem:resolvent-difference} with
\[
 W\getsI_n-\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}(m)^2,\qquad
 M\gets a(m)I_n-\beta A-\frac{2\beta^2}{n}mm^{\mathsf{T}},
\]
at each of the two values of $m$ required for the Lipschitzness bound, omitting the rank-one term for
$\overline Q$. The required input
bounds are
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{align*}
 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {W(m)-W(w)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}&\leqslant\frac2{\sqrt n}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}},\\
 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {M(m)-M(w)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}&\leqslant
      \left(\frac{2\beta^2}{\sqrt n}+\frac{4\beta^2}{n}\right)
                  \ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}}. \qedhere
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-DQ2-hQ2D}
In the setting of \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds}, for all
$m\in\mathcal S_A(c)$,
\[
 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {E_{\mathcal D_n}[D(m)(\widehat Q(m)^2-\overline Q(m)^2)D(m)]} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
                         \leqslant\frac{C_{\beta,c}}{\sqrt n}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Apply part (e) of \prettyref{lem:resolvent-difference}, keeping $W=D(m)^{-1}$
fixed and changing $M$ by $-2\beta^2mm^{\mathsf{T}}/n$.
The $\min\operatorname{Spec}(|M + W^{-1}|) > c$ condition is satisfied at both endpoints and $\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta M} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} = \ensuremath{\left\lVert {-2\beta^2mm^{\mathsf{T}}/n} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} \leqslant 2\beta^2/\sqrt n$.
Then contractivity of $E_{\mathcal D_n}$ concludes the bound.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:alg-diag-basic}
In the setting of \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds}, for $m,w\in\mathcal S_A(c)$,
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),ref=\thetheorem(\alph*),itemsep=0.1em]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-delta}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert \delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(m) \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C_{\beta,c}. \]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-delta}
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-diagonal-difference}
 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(m)-\delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(w)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
           \leqslant\frac{C_{\beta,c}}{\sqrt n}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
The proof is the same as \cite[Lemma 6.5]{davies2026potential}, using
\prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds} and
\prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz}. Both the diagonal projection and
$X\mapsto\operatorname{tr}_n(X)I_n$ are contractions in the relevant norms.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-delta-frob}
\label{lem:alg-diagonal-control-safe}
In the setting of \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds}, assume also that we are in the event $\Omega_{\beta,c}$.
Then for every $m\in\mathcal S_A(c)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-diagonal-small}
       \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(m)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\leqslant\frac{C_{\beta,c}}{\sqrt n}.
\end{equation}
The same bound, with every $\widehat Q$ replaced by $\overline Q$, holds for all
$m\in(-1,1)^n$ such that $D(m)\in\mathcal S_D$.
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
The proof of \cite[Lemma 6.6]{davies2026potential} is unchanged after
using the definition of $\mathcal S_A(c)$ and
\prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-DQ2-hQ2D} for the initial diagonal
estimate.
For $\overline Q$, skip the application of \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-DQ2-hQ2D}.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:alg-safe-drift}
In the setting of \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds}, assuming the event $\Omega_{\beta,c}$ defined in \prettyref{eq:alg-def-Omega} and with $f$ as in \eqref{eq:alg-f-definition}, the following bounds hold on $m \in \mathcal S_A(c)$ in Euclidean norm:
\allowdisplaybreaks
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),ref=\thetheorem(\alph*),itemsep=0.1em]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-f}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alg-f-Lipschitz}
                 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {f} \right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}}\leqslant C_{\beta,c}.
\end{equation}
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-m-f}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alg-mf-bound}
                 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-f(m)} \right\rVert_{2}}\leqslant C_{\beta,c}.
\end{equation}
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-drift}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alg-drift-bounded}
                 \ensuremath{\left\lVert {\widehat Q(m)(m-f(m))} \right\rVert_{2}}\leqslant C_{\beta,c}.
\end{equation}
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-dual-drift-1}
\[ \ensuremath{\left\lVert {D(m)\widehat Q(m)(m-f(m))} \right\rVert_{2}}\leqslant C_{\beta,c}. \]
\item \label{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-drift}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:alg-drift-Lipschitz}
       \ensuremath{\left\lVert {m\mapsto\widehat Q(m)(m-f(m))} \right\rVert_{\mathsf{Lip}}}\leqslant C_{\beta,c}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Use the proof of \cite[Lemma 6.7]{davies2026potential}, with all of the references to intermediate lemmas replaced with their analogs written above, with the same section-internal numbering. The identity expressing $f$ in terms of $\delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(m)$ is
\[
 f(m)=\left(I_n+\delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(m)
                         -\frac{2\beta^2}{n}\widehat Q(m)^2\right)m.
\]
In particular,
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(m)m} \right\rVert_{2}}\leqslant\sqrt n\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\delta_{\operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}}(m)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$, and
the same inequality applies to the difference of the diagonal errors
acting on any $w\in(-1,1)^n$.
All other matrix differences are bounded using
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert \cdot \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant\sqrt n\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\cdot} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$.
The remaining product estimates are unchanged.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:two-segment-W-path}
Fix $0<\beta<1$, $c>0$, and $A\in\mathrm{Sym}_n(\mathbb R)$.
Let $W_0,W_1\in\mathcal D_n((0,1])$ satisfy
\[
    W_j^{-1} \in \mathcal S_D,
    \qquad j=0,1,
\]
where $\mathcal S_D$ was defined in \prettyref{eq:alg-def-calSD}.
Then there exists a piecewise $C^1$ path
$W:[0,1]\to\mathcal D_n((0,1])$ with $W(0)=W_0$ and $W(1)=W_1$
such that
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-gap}
    W(t)^{-1} \in \mathcal S_D,
    \qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant1,
\end{equation}
and its normalized Schatten $2$-length satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-length}
    \int_0^1\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W'(t)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\,dt
    \leqslant\frac{2}{1-\beta^2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Let $\min(W_0,W_1)$ denote their coordinatewise minimum.
The path will proceed in a line segment from $W_0$ to $(1+s)^{-1}\min(W_0,W_1)$ for some scalar $s$ and then in another line segment to $W_1$.
Accordingly, denote the two line segments as
\[
    s:=\frac{\beta^2}{1-\beta^2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},
    \qquad
    W_j(t):=(1-t)W_j+\frac{t}{1+s}\min(W_0,W_1),
    \quad 0\leqslant t\leqslant1,\quad j=0,1.
\]
Both segments end at $(1+s)^{-1}\min(W_0,W_1)$ and satisfy
$0\prec W_j(t)\preceq W_j\preceqI_n$.
We now proceed with a few path derivative calculations to show that \prettyref{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-gap} holds throughout the line segments.
First,
\[
    -W_j'(t)=\frac{sW_j+W_j-\min(W_0,W_1)}{1+s}
    \succeq\frac{s}{1+s}W_j.
\]
By diagonality and $0\prec W_j(t)\preceq W_j\preceqI_n$,
\[
    \frac{d}{dt}W_j(t)^{-1}
    =W_j(t)^{-2}(-W_j'(t))
    \succeq\frac{s}{1+s}W_j(t)^{-2}W_j
    \succeq\frac{s}{1+s}I_n.
\]
Since $\operatorname{tr}_n(W_j)\leqslant1$, Cauchy--Schwarz for the normalized trace gives
\begin{align*}
    \operatorname{tr}_n[-W_j'(t)]
    &=\frac{s\operatorname{tr}_n(W_j)+\operatorname{tr}_n[W_j-\min(W_0,W_1)]}{1+s}\\
    &\leqslant\frac{s+\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_j-\min(W_0,W_1)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}}{1+s}
     \leqslant\frac{s+\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}}{1+s}.
\end{align*}
Combining these estimates yields
\begin{align*}
    \frac{d}{dt}\left(
        W_j(t)^{-1}+\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(W_j(t))I_n-\beta A
    \right)
    &\;\succeq\;
    \frac{(1-\beta^2)s-\beta^2\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}}{1+s}I_n
    \;=\;0.
\end{align*}
Integrating from $0$ to $t$ and using the $W_j^{-1} \in \mathcal S_D$ assumption gives
\[
    W_j(t)^{-1}+\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(W_j(t))I_n-\beta A
    \;\;\succeq\;\; W_j^{-1}+\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(W_j)I_n-\beta A
    \;\;\succeq\;\; cI_n,
\]
showing \eqref{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-gap} for both of the two segments.
Concatenate $W_0(t)$ with $W_1(t)$ traversed in reverse, and rescale the
parameter interval to $[0,1]$ to obtain the required piecewise $C^1$
path $W(t)$.
Since $\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_j} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\leqslant1$ and
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_j-\min(W_0,W_1)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\leqslant\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}$, its length satisfies
\begin{align*}
    \int_0^1\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W'(t)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\,dt
    &=\sum_{j=0}^1\int_0^1\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_j'(t)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\,dt\\
    &\leqslant\frac{2\bigl(s+\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\bigr)}{1+s}\\
    &=\frac{2}{(1-\beta^2)(1+s)}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
     \leqslant\frac{2}{1-\beta^2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}},
\end{align*}
which proves \eqref{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-length}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:omega-safe-Lipschitz}
In the setting of \prettyref{lem:alg-safe-drift}, let
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-omega}
 \omega(m):=\frac12\bigl(\mathsf{Tr}[\widehat{Q}(m)]+\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m} \right\rVert_{2}}^2\bigr),
                  \qquad (\forall m\in\mathcal S_A(c))
\end{equation}
as in \ref{d:JE-lip}.
Then there is an $L_\omega=C_{\beta,c}<\infty$ such that
\[
       |\omega(m)-\omega(w)|\leqslant L_\omega\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}}
                       \qquad(\forall m,w\in\mathcal S_A(c)).
\]
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Fix $A$. 
We will first prove the Lipschitz bound for
\[
 \overline\omega(m):=\frac12(\mathsf{Tr}\overline Q(m)+\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m} \right\rVert_{2}}^2)
       =\frac n2+\frac12(\mathsf{Tr}\overline Q(m)-\mathsf{Tr} W),
       \qquad W=D(m)^{-1},
\]
and then add the rank-one term back in at the end.
With $A$ held fixed, we use the right-hand expression to regard
$\overline\omega$ as a function of $W\in\mathcal D_n((0,1])$ wherever
the following inverse exists:
\[
 D=W^{-1},\qquad a=\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(W),\qquad
 \overline Q=\bigl(W^{-1}+\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(W)I_n-\beta A\bigr)^{-1}.
\]
Differentiating with respect to $W$ in a diagonal direction $H$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-baromega-W-differential}
 d_W\!\left[\overline{\omega}\right][H] = 
 d_W\!\left[\frac12(\mathsf{Tr}\overline Q-\mathsf{Tr} W)\right][H]
 =\frac12\mathsf{Tr}\!\left[
   \left(E_{\mathcal D_n}[D\overline Q^2D]
       -(1+\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(\overline Q^2))I_n\right)H\right].
\end{equation}
For every $W$ satisfying \eqref{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-gap},
\prettyref{lem:alg-diagonal-control-safe} gives
\[
 \left\|E_{\mathcal D_n}[D\overline Q^2D]
       -(1+\beta^2\operatorname{tr}_n(\overline Q^2))I_n\right\|_F
 \leqslant C_{\beta,c}.
\]
Cauchy--Schwarz for the unnormalized trace in
\eqref{eq:alg-baromega-W-differential} therefore yields, for all $W$ satisfying \eqref{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-gap} and diagonal $H$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:alg-baromega-W-derivative-bound}
 \left|d_W\!\left[\overline{\omega}\right][H]\right|
 \leqslant C_{\beta,c}\|H\|_F.
\end{equation}
Fix $m,w\in\mathcal S_A(c)$ and set
$W_0=D(m)^{-1}$ and $W_1=D(w)^{-1}$, which implies $W_0^{-1},W_1^{-1} \in \mathcal S_D$.
Then let $W(t)$ be the path supplied by \prettyref{lem:two-segment-W-path} between those two endpoints,
so that \eqref{eq:alg-baromega-W-derivative-bound} applies along this path.
Since $\overline\omega$ depends only on $D(m)^{-1}$, integrating that bound
and using \eqref{eq:alg-baromega-W-path-length} gives
\begin{align}
    |\overline\omega(m)-\overline\omega(w)|
    &\leqslant C_{\beta,c}\sqrt n\int_0^1\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W'(t)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}\,dt \notag\\
    &\leqslant\frac{2C_{\beta,c}\sqrt n}{1-\beta^2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
     \leqslant C_{\beta,c}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}},
\label{eq:alg-baromega-endpoint}
\end{align}
where the last inequality uses
\[
    \sqrt n\ensuremath{\left\lVert {W_1-W_0} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}
    =\left(\sum_{i=1}^n(m_i^2-w_i^2)^2\right)^{1/2}
    \leqslant2\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}}.
\]

Finally, each $\widehat Q-\overline Q$ has rank at most one, so their difference
between two endpoints has rank at most two. Therefore
\begin{align*}
 &\left|\mathsf{Tr}\bigl[(\widehat Q(m)-\overline Q(m))
                       -(\widehat Q(w)-\overline Q(w))\bigr]\right|\\
 &\quad\leqslant\sqrt2\left(
       \|\widehat Q(m)-\widehat Q(w)\|_F
       +\|\overline Q(m)-\overline Q(w)\|_F\right)
       \leqslant C_{\beta,c}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}},
\end{align*}
by \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-Q}, applied to $\widehat Q$ and
$\overline Q$ at $m,w\in\mathcal S_A(c)$.
Combining this with \eqref{eq:alg-baromega-endpoint} proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:alg-desiderata}
Assume we are in the events $\{\ensuremath{\left\lVert A \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant 3\}$ and $\Omega_{\beta,c}$ as defined in \prettyref{eq:alg-def-Omega}.
Then the following hold with constants independent
of $n$ and $A$.
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=0.45em,label=\textup{(d\arabic*)},
                 ref={\thetheorem\,(d\arabic*)},start=0]
\setcounter{enumi}{2}
\item \label{cor:alg-desiderata-d3}
For every $m\in\mathcal S_A(c)$,
$\ensuremath{\left\lVert {f(m)-m} \right\rVert_{2}}^2\leqslant C_{\beta,c}^2$.
\item \label{cor:alg-desiderata-d4}
\begin{enumerate}[itemsep=0.1em]
\item
There is a constant $L\leqslant C_{\beta,c}$ such that
$\|\widehat Q(m)-\widehat Q(w)\|_F\leqslant L\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}}$ for $m,w\in\mathcal S_A(c)$.
        \item There exists a constant $L_{\mathrm{drift}}$  such that for all $m, w\in \mathcal S_A(c_{{\mathrm{TAP}}})$, with $f$ defined as in \prettyref{eq:alg-f-definition},
    \[
    \left\Vert {\widehat{Q}(m)(m-f(m)) - \widehat{Q}(w)(w-f(w))}\right\Vert_2 \leqslant L_{\mathrm{drift}}\left\Vert {m - w}\right\Vert_2.
    \]
\end{enumerate}
\item \label{cor:alg-desiderata-d5}
 Let
        \[\omega(m):=\frac12\bigl(\mathsf{Tr}[\widehat{Q}(m)]+\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m} \right\rVert_{2}}^2\bigr).\]
        Then there is an $L_\omega<\infty$ such that
\[
       |\omega(m)-\omega(w)|\leqslant L_\omega\ensuremath{\left\lVert {m-w} \right\rVert_{2}}
                       \qquad(\forall m,w\in\mathcal S_A(c)).
\]
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
\par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Parts (d3)--(d4).}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

As in \cite[Corollary 6.14]{davies2026potential}, these follow in order
from \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-bounds-m-f},
\prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-Q},
and \prettyref{lem:alg-resolvent-lipschitz-drift}.
.
For (d4), convert normalized Schatten $2$-norm to Frobenius norm.

 
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part (d5).}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 
 This is \prettyref{lem:omega-safe-Lipschitz}. \qedhere
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:localized-sampler}
Fix $\beta>0$, $\varepsilon(\beta) = \frac{1}{C\overline{\beta}^2\log(e\overline{\beta})}$ with $C>0$ some large enough universal constant and $\overline{\beta} = \max\{1,\beta\}$, and $k\leqslant \varepsilon(\beta)n$.
Then, with probability $1-o_n(1)$, 
for any $ 0 < \beta' < \beta$ and $0<\varepsilon < 1/2$, uniformly over $y\in \mathbb R^n$, letting $\sigma_0=\operatorname{sign}(y) \in \{-1,1\}^n$, \cite[Algorithm 2]{kumar2026high} run for $2\log\left(\frac{16}{\varepsilon^2(\beta')}\right)$ annealing levels, with $\mathsf{poly}(n)\log(1/\varepsilon)$ Markov chain steps for each level,
gives a sample that is $\varepsilon$ close in TV distance from $\mu_{\beta' A, y}|_{B(\sigma_0, \varepsilon n)}$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Define the diagonal matrix $R_{y} = \operatorname{\mathsf{diag}}(\operatorname{sign}(y))$ and set $x = -R_{y}\sigma$ for any $\sigma \in B(\operatorname{sign}(y),\varepsilon(\beta')n)$. Clearly, then, $\sigma$ can be in the wedge around $y$ if, and only if, the number of positive signs in $x$ are no more than $\varepsilon(\beta')n$. This bijection between centering on $y$ and $0^n$ allows the measure on the wedge to be rewritten as
    \[
        \mu_{y}(x) \propto \exp\left\{\frac{\beta}{2}\left(x^\mathsf{T}(R_y JR_y)x - (R_yy)^\mathsf{T} x\right)\right\}\,,
    \]
    where we use the distributional equivalence $R_yJR_y \overset{d}{=} \mathsf{GOE}(n)$. Denote the new parameters $J = \frac{1}{2}R_y AR_y$ for $A\sim\mathsf{GOE}(n)$ and $h = -\frac{2R_yy}{\beta}$. Note that the \cite[Proof of Theorem 7]{kumar2026high} applies verbatim with the interaction matrix $R_y J R_y$ due to the distributional equivalence before since the Dobrushin-type bounds based on \cite[Lemma 8]{kumar2026high} remain unchanged. Note that we choose $\varepsilon(\beta) = \frac{1}{C\overline{\beta}^2\log(e\overline{\beta})} \leqslant \frac{1}{C\overline{\beta'}^2\log(e\overline{\beta'})} = \varepsilon(\beta')$ so that the validity of \cite[Theorem 4]{kumar2026high} holds when invoking \cite[Corollary 5]{kumar2026high}, and we can get the mixing to hold simultaneously on the same event for $A$ for every $0<\beta'<\beta$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:resolvent-difference}
Let $W_0,W_1\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}$ be invertible symmetric matrices,
and let $M_0,M_1\in\mathbb C^{n\times n}$.
Suppose that, for $j=0,1$,
\[
    \min\operatorname{Spec}(|M_j+W_j^{-1}|)\geqslant\gamma,
    \qquad
    \ensuremath{\left\lVert M_j \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}}\leqslant C_1,
\]
where $\gamma>0$ and $C_1\geqslant0$.
Let $C_2:=(\gamma+C_1)\gamma^{-1}$.

    Let $D(W) := W^{-1}$, $X(W,M) := M + D(W)$, and $R(W,M) := X(W,M)^{-1}$ whenever the stated inverses exist.
    
    Let $\Delta$ be the difference operator operating on functions of $W$ and $M$, so that $\Delta f := f(W_1, M_1) - f(W_0,M_0)$.
    Let $T \in \mathbb C^{n \times n}$ be fixed and take all exponents $k$ below to be integers.
    Then:
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*), ref=\thetheorem(\alph*)]
        \item \[\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta R} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} \leqslant \gamma^{-2}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta M} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} + C_2^2\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta W} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.\]
        \label{lem:resolvent-difference-dR}
        \item For $k \geqslant 1$, \[\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta(R^k)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} \leqslant k\gamma^{-(k+1)}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta M} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} + kC_2^2\gamma^{-(k-1)}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta W} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.\]
        \label{lem:resolvent-difference-dRk}
        \item \[\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta(DR)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} \leqslant C_2\gamma^{-1}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta M} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} + C_1C_2^2\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta W} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.\]
        \label{lem:resolvent-difference-dDR}
        \item For $k \geqslant 1$,
        \[\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta(DR^k)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} \leqslant kC_2\gamma^{-k}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta M} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} + (kC_2-1)C_2^2\gamma^{-(k-2)}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta W} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.\]
        \label{lem:resolvent-difference-dDRk}
        \item For $k \geqslant 2$, \[\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta(DR^kD)} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} \leqslant kC_2^2\gamma^{-(k-1)}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta M} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}} + (kC_2-2)C_2^3\gamma^{-(k-3)}\ensuremath{\left\lVert {\Delta W} \right\rVert_{L^{2}}}.\]
        \label{lem:resolvent-difference-dDRkD}
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
For $f$ a matrix-valued function of $W$ and $M$, we define the evaluation operators $f_1 := f(W_1, M_1)$ and $f_0 := f(W_0, M_0)$ so that $\Delta f = f_1 - f_0$.
    Then we have the discrete non-commutative sum, product, power, and inverse rules.
 Explicitly,
 \begin{itemize}
     \item $\Delta(f+g) = \Delta f + \Delta g$,
     \item $\Delta(fg) = f_1(\Delta g) + (\Delta f)g_0$,
     \item $\Delta(f^k) = \sum_{i = 1}^{k}f_1^{i-1}(\Delta f)f_0^{k-i}$,
     \item $\Delta(f^{-1}) = -f_1^{-1}(\Delta f)f_0^{-1}$ (also known as the resolvent identity).
 \end{itemize}

    Note by the resolvent identity, $\Delta D = -D_1(\Delta W)D_0$ and $\Delta X = \Delta M - D_1(\Delta W)D_0$.
    By the argument of \cite[Lemma B.1]{davies2026potential}, we have $\ensuremath{\left\lVert R^k \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant \gamma^{-k}$ and $\ensuremath{\left\lVert DR^{k+1} \right\rVert_\mathsf{op}} \leqslant C_2\gamma^{-k}$, which we will use in every following part.
    We will also repeatedly use the non-commutative H\"older inequality.

     
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part \ref{lem:resolvent-difference-dR}.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 
 We apply the just-mentioned operator norm bounds after computing the derivative
    \[\Delta R = -R_1(\Delta X)R_0 = -R_1(\Delta M)R_0 + (RD)_1(\Delta W)(DR)_0.\]

     
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part \ref{lem:resolvent-difference-dRk}.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces 

    We use the discrete non-commutative power rule
    \[\Delta(R^k) = \sum_{j=1}^{k} R_1^{j-1}(\Delta R)R_0^{k-j},\]
    then apply \prettyref{lem:resolvent-difference-dR} and the same operator norm bounds from before.

     
  \par\addvspace{\smallskipamount} 
  \noindent\textit{Part \ref{lem:resolvent-difference-dDR}.}\hspace{0.5em}\ignorespaces
